Abstract: Most AI assistants respond only when prompted, remaining silent even when events in the video stream offer meaningful opportunities for conversation. To move beyond purely user-driven interaction, we present AURA 2.0, a streaming video large language model that introduces three core upgrades to AURA. The key advance is Model-Initiated Conversation (MIC), which enables the model to identify topics worth discussing in the evolving visual stream and initiate conversations at appropriate moments without a user query. Stream-Aware Tool Calling (SATC) enables the model to invoke external tools using the current visual and conversational context and incorporate returned information into the ongoing interaction. Interactive Visual Grounding (IVG) supports visual grounding in both user inputs and model outputs. The model can identify objects marked by users on video frames and produce frame-aligned bounding boxes to link its responses to specific objects and regions. To develop these capabilities, we construct a dedicated streaming interaction dataset and adopt a three-stage training scheme to integrate the new behaviors with AURA's existing streaming interaction abilities. We further incorporate Long-Horizon Interaction Memory (LHIM) to maintain contextual continuity over extended video streams. Evaluations on public streaming benchmarks show that AURA 2.0 achieves leading performance among open-source streaming models, while targeted evaluations further validate its new interaction capabilities. We open-source the model weights and inference code.

</details>

Abstract: Large foundation models such as GPT-6 Astra and Claude Fable 5.1 have recently been shown to control robots directly from visual observations. They nevertheless remain brittle on long-horizon, contact-rich tasks in real-world environments. Further progress depends on two complementary resources: demonstrations of human behavior that supervise how tasks are performed, and interactive environments in which policies can be rolled out, evaluated, and improved in closed loop. Language models have advanced along well-characterized scaling laws; the corresponding question for physical AI is how capability scales with environments and behaviors, which first requires identifying which data to scale and how to produce and reuse such data continuously. To address this question, we introduce Ropedia Xperience 2, comprising 1,589 released metric-scale 3D scans and a collection of 4,230 egocentric recordings of 74 tasks (131 hours). We further show that each capture can be repurposed for robot learning: scenes are converted into physically interactive simulation environments, human motion is retargeted to whole-body robot trajectories, and additional robot behaviors are obtained through virtual teleoperation and automated long-horizon trajectory generation. We propose this joint capture of environments and behaviors as a gold standard for physical AI data collection, laying a data foundation for establishing scaling laws over environments and behaviors.

Abstract: Change detection in remote sensing conventionally relies on spatially aligned bi-temporal imagery, which may be unavailable or unreliable in practical scenarios. Recent single-temporal approaches address this limitation by learning structural evidence, such as building regions, from a labeled source dataset and transferring it to unseen target imagery using only the current image. However, adaptation to the source dataset can over-specialize pretrained features and reduce their cross-dataset generalization, a risk that this paper argues is higher when the feature adaptation is parameterized independently of the states used for prediction. To address this issue, the current study introduces SALT, a State-Aligned Low-Rank Transfer framework. SALT keeps a DINOv3 backbone frozen and learns negative and positive structural states that both score each patch and, through State-Aligned Low-Rank Adaptation (SALA), span the rank-2 subspace in which patch features are corrected. Directional State Alignment supervises these corrections to raise the evidence of positive patches and lower that of negative ones, while Geometry Preservation limits changes to the pairwise relations among pretrained patch features. Experiments on LEVIR-CD and the WHU aerial building dataset show that, at the pixel level and in both transfer directions, SALT outperforms four baselines reproduced under a common source-only protocol, improving over the strongest reproduced baselines by 9.45 and 5.79 points in pixel-level AP and by 27.69 and 14.25 points in IoU (LEVIR-CD to WHU and WHU to LEVIR-CD, respectively), with only 0.068M trainable parameters and 40.2 ms per image. Abstract: Merging independently fine-tuned checkpoints into a single multi-task model requires no data, no labels, and no gradient steps. In practice the task vectors of different tasks interfere, and the standard remedies--averaging, magnitude pruning, sign election--operate entry by entry, ignoring that each delta induces a low-dimensional update on the hidden features it acts upon. We recast merging as a subspace interference problem. For every two-dimensional weight tensor we take the top-r right-singular subspace of each task's delta--the input directions along which that task moves the representation most--and remove from each task vector the component lying in the union of the other tasks' subspaces. The resulting Joint Null-Space Projection (JNSP) is closed-form and order-independent, needs no forward pass, and is defined entirely by the checkpoint set. On eight CLIP image-classification tasks JNSP merges ViT-B/16 experts to 89.45% average accuracy without using a single image, 11.88 points above the strongest parameter-wise training-free baseline (TIES-Merging, 77.57%), 14.22 above Task Arithmetic, and 0.98 above WUDI's data-free consensus merge (88.47%). The gain holds across three backbones on the independently trained TALL release (+10.8, +12.6, +9.1 over TIES for ViT-B/16, ViT-B/32, ViT-L/14) and replicates there at 89.20%; controls confirm the source--a single shared subspace instead of per-task ones loses 2.9 points, and a sequential variant collapses to 69.6%. On RoBERTa-large the same projection underperforms plain task arithmetic, which we report rather than hide. Code is available at https://github.com.

</details>

We present STeP-C (Spatio-Temporal Predictive Compression), a neural compression framework that decouples spatial encoding, temporal prediction, and entropy-optimized residual coding, trained end-to-end with a rate-distortion objective motivated by conditional entropy reduction. Critically, we identify entropy coding as the dominant bottleneck for streaming deployment. We address it through GPU-side symbol computation, cached probability tables, and parallel GIL-free arithmetic coding, together with FP16 inference and batched entropy coding, enabling a full real-time pipeline built around a GPU ring buffer. We validate this pipeline on a live DAS testbed, achieving a 98.85% file size reduction at 31.04 dB full-band PSNR and an average throughput of 535 MB/s, exceeding the 324 MB/s requirement for 1 kHz, 81 km DAS streams. We further show that STeP-C substantially outperforms conventional codecs at practical streaming bitrates, can be applied to unseen channel ranges within the same acquisition system without retraining, and largely maintains downstream detector consistency, with 96.62% high-confidence class agreement and 100% Jaccard similarity on detected event types.

</details>

Abstract: The proliferation of user-generated video content has made accurate and robust no-reference video quality assessment (NR-VQA) a highly important challenge. Trained solely on LSVQ and evaluated without any fine-tuning, HiVE-VQA outperforms 17 state-of-the-art (SOTA) methods in SROCC and PLCC on both intra-dataset (LSVQ-test, LSVQ-1080p) and cross-dataset (KoNViD-1k, LIVE-VQC) benchmarks. These results indicate that hierarchical, depth-wise fusion of features from a single frozen video foundation model provides a more effective and efficient basis for NR-VQA than task-specific multi-network fusion. Abstract: Reference-frame stabilization registers every frame of a hovering-drone video onto one reference frame, keeping object positions comparable. A two-part benchmark measures it and the object masking meant to prevent it. A static protocol scores ten pipelines under exactly known homographies on 4K aerial frames, tuned on a site-disjoint split at a matched resolution and feature budget. A dynamic protocol scores them at an offline and a real-time budget on hovers selected from the video alone to span the amount and coherence of moving foreground, plus a stationary control. It measures background alignment, object position error and the share of a moving group's displacement an estimate follows, against a pseudo-ground-truth reference audited with manually annotated ground control points, which also score every candidate. On static scenes, tuned AKAZE, RootSIFT, SIFT and BRISK lead, retuning the matcher thresholds raises the mean average accuracy (mAA) by at most 0.0062, and masking costs less than 0.005. Abstract: Distribution matching distillation (DMD) has emerged as a leading paradigm for distilling diffusion models into few-step students, where an auxiliary fake score model tracks the student's distribution and the discrepancy between the teacher and fake predictions drives the student toward the teacher. In multi-step distillation, although only the student's final output is delivered, existing methods train the fake model on intermediate states or across all denoising steps. Consequently, the student's update signal becomes entangled with intermediate distributions, which can distort the matching direction and remain nonzero even after the student's final outputs already match the teacher distribution. To resolve this mismatch, we propose Endpoint Distribution Matching Distillation (eDMD), which trains a single shared fake model exclusively on the student's final outputs and uses it to correct every denoising step. Our theoretical analysis proves that this endpoint reference guarantees that the distribution matching term vanishes if and only if the student's final output distribution matches the teacher's, regardless of intermediate distributions. Beyond the fake reference, we further strengthen teacher supervision through a precise teacher (PT) that replaces single-step guided predictions with short multi-step trajectories on the final noise interval, paired with a calibrated query density for intermediate steps. Experiments on SD3-Medium, FLUX.2-klein (4B and 9B), and Qwen-Image-2.1 demonstrate that eDMD consistently outperforms existing distribution matching methods for 4-step generation.

</details>

Abstract: Hyperspectral images (HSI) possess discriminating spectral characteristics with respect to land utilization due to high-dimensional information. Recently, unsupervised representation learning using graph neural networks has unveiled these utilization-specific complex spectral relations. This article has three main contributions. First, it proposes an index, the Mean Absolute Local Neighborhood Inverse Spectral Index (MALNISI), which measures the similarity between two pixels based on their neighborhood information. The index is used to construct a weighted adjacency matrix, giving the HSI a graph structure. Second, it proposes an upgraded Graph Attention Network that learns compact latent representations of hyperspectral data from the constructed graph. In this network, an enhanced multi-head attention mechanism incorporates the MALNISI weights as an auxiliary feature to emphasize learned attention between neighboring pixels. To alleviate over-smoothing and preserve fine-grained spectral details, the architecture integrates residual connections, skip projections, and feature normalization, which together enhance representation capability, stabilize learning, and accelerate convergence. Third, it proposes a weighted contrastive loss function that incorporates neighborhood-aware MALNISI between connected pixels, encouraging strongly connected pixels in the high-dimensional space to remain close in the latent space, and weakly connected pixels to stay apart. The resulting discriminative low-dimensional embeddings are then clustered using spectral clustering. Experimental analysis on six benchmark HSI datasets demonstrates the efficiency and necessity of the three aforementioned contributions, and the comparative study highlights superior performance over fourteen state-of-the-art approaches.

</details>

Abstract: Humans naturally parse visual scenes into coherent individual objects rather than scattered features, a capability cognitive science calls object binding. While recent work shows that pretrained Vision Transformers (ViTs) intrinsically acquire this ability, how native self-attention computations mechanistically keep object instances separate remains unresolved. By studying this across eight pretrained ViTs, we found that instance separation is expressed more strongly in attention routing than in token content: selected query-key relations distinguish individual objects more reliably than value similarity or residual representations. Inside heads, patches belonging to one individual tend to read from a common image region, which we term their attention address. Visually distinct parts of one person share an address, whereas identical duplicated instances receive separate, movable addresses. These shared destinations arise in part because query interactions with the spatial structure of keys direct attention toward consistent regions for one object and elsewhere for another. In DINOv2 and most other backbones we tested, editing this query component at the selected head redirects its attention address toward another individual. Grouping patches by this query component separates same-category instances, reaching .615 FG-ARI versus .289 for the same clustering of final-layer features, which tend to merge them.

Abstract: Interactive video-to-video applications require real-time generation while maintaining long-range temporal consistency. However, recent methods achieve speed by restricting attention to a fixed-length causal window. This limits long-range consistency whenever relevant history falls outside the context window. To address this limitation, we introduce vid2vid-long, a correspondence-based approach grounded in a simple observation. Video-to-video translation provides an explicit cross-frame reference: the regions that match in the input video should match in the output video too. Concretely, we compute patch-level correspondences in the input, guiding retrieval of previously generated outputs in the corresponding regions, and condition the model on these patches through correspondence cross-attention. Our experiments demonstrate that vid2vid-long consistently improves long-range temporal consistency across recent video models while preserving generation quality and real-time performance.

Abstract: Local Binary Patterns (LBP) remain among the most widely used texture descriptors, from industrial inspection to medical imaging. Yet their radius, sampling angles, color transform and thresholds are fixed by hand for each dataset. We present FLEX-LBP, which learns these parameters by backpropagation while every forward pass computes exact binary codes and hard histogram counts. A straight-through estimator acts only in the backward pass, so the deployed descriptor is an interpretable hard-binary LBP with 90 learnable parameters and unchanged inference cost. In a controlled ablation on six color texture benchmarks, the component worth learning is dataset-dependent. One configuration, applied unchanged everywhere, learns the geometry and thresholds with the color transform frozen. It improves the nearest-neighbor accuracy of the identical fixed descriptor by 3.55 points on average over five seeds, significantly on five of six benchmarks and never significantly worse. With a linear discriminant readout, it gains 0.9 points; the fully learned descriptor gains 2.7 and has the highest mean. FLEX-LBP is more accurate than five classical LBP-family descriptors, PCANet and a neural LBP on five of six benchmarks. Its mean nearest-neighbor accuracy exceeds that of frozen ImageNet ResNet-50 features (23.5 million parameters). On the Kather colorectal cancer histology benchmark, the fully learned descriptor reaches 93.8%, significantly above the best classical descriptor (Completed LBP, 92.8%). The learned radii adapt in directions that recur across seeds. Noise drowns single-pixel comparisons and illuminant change flips inter-channel ones; median sampling or gray-world normalization restores 96-97%. The code is available as a drop-in PyTorch layer at https://github.com/medalimoussa/FLEX-LBP.

Abstract: Spatial intelligence requires connecting visual observations to the geometry, objects, and persistent state of an environment. Images alone leave these relationships implicit, while disconnected annotations make it difficult to study appearance, camera motion, and scene changes under a common reference. We introduce Spatial Foundry, a collection of 5,280 furnished residential 3D scene configurations with editable assets and aligned visual, geometric, and temporal data. Its modalities include floor plans, perspective and panoramic RGB, metric depth, point clouds, meshes, instance-level 3D bounding boxes, and camera trajectories paired with video. Shared scene coordinates link observations across views and time; editable source scenes enable additional camera paths and controlled object changes. We describe the data representation and construction workflow, and discuss applications in floor-plan-conditioned 3D generation, geometric reconstruction, instance-level perception, camera-conditioned video generation, shared world modeling, and scene editing. Controlled camera and scene variations support spatial perception and world-model research. The public project page provides videos and an interactive colored point-cloud preview.

Abstract: Given a set of LEGO parts and an image of an assembled object, our task is to infer the pose of each part to assemble the object. In contrast with traditional 3D reconstruction, doing so requires satisfying discrete physical constraints, which provide structure but render the problem combinatorial and highly sensitive to small pose errors. To learn a prior over how parts fit together, we train a set-conditioned SE(3) flow model on procedurally generated assemblies. We then finetune this model to inject image conditioning, using 1% as much data. However, while the learned flow captures global structure, it does not enforce connectivity. To address this, we introduce an analytic connectivity-field guidance to pull predictions toward connected assemblies at test time. Through this, we demonstrate improved build quality with model size, pretraining, and guidance. We make our models and data available for research purposes at https://kulits.github.io/BrickFlow/

Abstract: Video diffusion models are able to generate samples with high-quality appearance and textural qualities. However, content produced by these methods is known to exhibit significant physical errors. This is because, in a classical video generation setting, all physical reasoning must take place simultaneously and exclusively within the diffusion process.We propose to resolve these issues by redistributing the task of physical reasoning onto a language model-backed keyframe generation module, which allows for explicit reasoning in language, and fine-grained control over the appearance of the video at important moments in the video. We then condition a video generation model with these keyframes, reducing the role of the diffusion model to the simpler task of interpolating between physically-correct intermediate states, rather than inferring an entire sequence with diffusion. Results demonstrate that our approach significantly outperforms existing approaches, achieving state-of-the-art performance on the Physics-IQ Verified benchmark and PhyGenBench.

We propose Test-Time Baking (TTB), a strategy that learns to bake global scene information from cross-view attention into a lightweight MLP, enabling fast and high-fidelity novel-view decoding. The first key is to repurpose the fast-weight MLP of TTT to learn the cross-view attention mapping explicitly from its input-output pairs, rather than replacing attention entirely, achieving the same rendering efficiency as TTT while delivering substantially higher quality. Abstract: How multimodal language models should encode visual input remains up for debate. Most models use a pretrained vision encoder, while some recent recipes train the encoder from scratch with the language model or remove it entirely. Vision encoders bundle together three design choices: a visual prior from upstream pretraining, dedicated vision capacity, and pre-fusion placement of that capacity (i.e., before the multimodal backbone). Because published models also differ in data, architecture, and compute, comparing them cannot isolate the effect of each choice. We disentangle these choices via a controlled framework where we pretrain all models from scratch on common data and compare them on visual question answering and language benchmarks at matched lifetime compute. We find that the visual prior is not necessary; an encoder trained jointly from scratch is on par with a pretrained one, without a separate training stage. What matters instead is dedicated vision capacity: an encoder-free model trails on visual understanding even when its backbone is enlarged by the encoder's parameter count. Yet that capacity need not precede multimodal fusion, as vision-only modules added in-fusion to early backbone layers perform comparably to a pre-fusion encoder and can lower time to first token. These results also hold across backbone sizes, when encoder pretraining is treated as free, and when starting from a pretrained language model. Overall, our findings favor dedicated vision capacity trained jointly with the backbone and placed early within it.

</details>

This compression made training tractable, but now limits high-resolution, controllable, and interactive generation -- doubling resolution quadruples token count, conditions apply to groups of frames rather than individual ones, and each response must wait for the rest of its multi-frame block. Abstract: In this work, we propose a decentralized terrain-aware multi-drone Thompson Sampling algorithm tailored for search and rescue (SAR) applications. A probability distribution of locating a lost person is informed by terrain data, using established SAR models (displacement ring, elevation, watershed, dispersion angle, and track offset) derived from historical SAR case data on lost person behavior. Exploration is conducted by a team of drones using Thompson Sampling, where candidate actions are selected by drawing samples from this probability distribution, represented by a per-cell Gaussian posterior whose mean and variance are updated through Bayesian inference. This approach balances exploration of uncertain regions with exploitation of high-probability areas to locate a lost person. Observation uncertainty is incorporated through fractional forest coverage, which measures the percentage of an observation occluded by tree coverage and accounts for reduced detection performance in densely vegetated areas. Simulations using real SAR case data (YOSAR) evaluate the proposed method against a coverage planner and a probabilistic active search method in search regions of 1.5 and 3.8 square kilometers, with 5, 10, 15, and 20 agents flying at 50 km/hr. We demonstrate that our method achieves a success rate of approximately 87% within 60 to 247 seconds in the 1.5 square kilometer region, and success rates of approximately 75% to 91% within 161 to 466 seconds in the 3.8 square kilometer region, across different agent configurations.

</details>

Abstract: Soft robots built from active materials can directly convert environmental stimuli into motion, offering a route to energy autonomy and embodied control. However, environmental energy is generally harvested at low power, whereas rapid, mechanically demanding actions require high mechanical output power. Reconciling this mismatch while sustaining self-regulated cyclic actuation is challenging, particularly at the subgram scale, where limited payload capacity constrains the integration of conventional power and control hardware. Here, we present SunLightBots, a class of subgram, sunlight-powered, untethered soft robots that achieve autonomous locomotion under continuous illumination near 1 Sun (100 mW/cm2). Their operation is enabled by a self-resetting snapping (SRS) mechanism that integrates liquid crystal elastomers (LCEs) with snap-through instability to accumulate solar energy supplied at low power, release it as rapid, high-power motion, and reset through photothermal-mechanical feedback. Through coordinated optimization of the LCE actuation temperature, photothermal conversion, actuation stress, and structural energy barrier, the system operates near 1 Sun and achieves a peak mass-specific mechanical power of 89 W/kg. At this irradiance, the optimized LCE generates an actuation stress of 0.1 MPa at approximately 60 deg C. SunLightBots demonstrate sustained swimming, crawling, and repeated impulsive jumping. Under 1.5 Suns, the swimmer advances more than three body lengths per cycle. This work provides a materials-mechanics route toward autonomous soft robots powered entirely by the environment.

</details>

Abstract: Prosthetic bypasses allow able-bodied individuals to walk with a prosthesis, enabling safe early-stage testing of new prosthetic devices before clinical trials. However, existing transtibial bypasses impose constraints, such as a contralateral shoe lift or a lateral offset of the prosthesis, and their effects on gait remain poorly documented. We present an open-source transtibial prosthetic bypass that preserves part of the sagittal knee mobility without these constraints. Six able-bodied participants familiarized themselves with the bypass for approximately 10~min, then walked on a treadmill with and without it, and joint kinematics were recorded with motion capture and then extracted with OpenSim. Abstract: Conventional vector-field-based motion planners for nonholonomic robots consist of two steps: first designing a nominal vector field and then controlling the robot to follow it to approach the target. However, a nonholonomic robot cannot follow the vector field instantaneously and may collide with obstacles, due to the inevitable mismatch between the robot's initial heading and the vector field's direction. This paper proposes a diffeomorphic vector-field alignment (DIVAL) method, which aligns the vector field's direction with the robot's initial heading so the robot can follow the vector field from the start. Specifically, the nominal vector field is locally reshaped by applying a diffeomorphic deformation to the workspace, such that the reshaped vector field has the same direction as the robot's heading at the initial position and coincides with the nominal vector field outside the deformed region. Since DIVAL eliminates the initial heading mismatch, the nonholonomic robot can maintain heading alignment with the reshaped vector field throughout the motion. This heading alignment further ensures that the robot preserves the nominal vector field's properties, including obstacle avoidance, convergence to the target, and smooth control signals. Numerical simulations provide vector-field-tracking baselines where the nominal vector fields are reshaped with DIVAL. Compared to the original baselines, the proposed method achieves collision-free convergence in all trials and avoids control discontinuities. Hardware experiments on a ground vehicle further demonstrate collision-free convergence across different targets and initial conditions under real-world disturbances.

</details>

Abstract: Extending humanoid interaction generation to new environments remains limited by the need for prebuilt 3D scene assets. Reconstructing scenes directly from single RGB images offers a way to overcome this limitation, but introduces a key challenge in maintaining geometric consistency throughout motion generation and transfer. Visually plausible human motions may be misaligned with the reconstructed scene, while retargeting can introduce additional support errors due to differences between human and robot embodiments.
  We present a unified pipeline that transforms a single RGB image into a humanoid motion reference. It reconstructs scene geometry from the input image, generates a human interaction conditioned on the reconstructed scene, and retargets the motion to a humanoid robot. To maintain geometric consistency throughout this process, we incorporate two complementary refinement stages before and after embodiment transfer. Before retargeting, Joint Metric Interaction Refinement (JMIR) refines human motion alignment with the registered metric scene while preserving the generated interaction structure. Following retargeting, Contact Guided Residual Retargeting (CGRR) corrects robot specific support errors to restore the intended support relationships. Together, these stages produce motion references that are consistent with the reconstructed scene and can be followed by multiple pretrained humanoid trackers without scene specific policy fine tuning. Compared with GRAIL, our method reduces refinement time from 480 s to 87 s, yielding a speedup of 5.5 times. The complete pipeline achieves a success rate of 89% in simulation and 70% across 50 physical trials on the Unitree G1, demonstrating its effectiveness in generating executable humanoid motion references from single RGB images.

Abstract: Imitation policies can perform complex manipulation, yet small execution errors can accumulate and cause failure. Refinement can correct these errors, but existing approaches often require additional on-policy learning or costly online planning. Our key insight is that a pretrained policy already supplies a useful nominal behavior: refinement can focus on predicting how small action corrections change its outcome. We therefore learn this local response in a form that is cheap to optimize. Our \textit{action-affine world model} (A\textsuperscript{2}WM) learns local action sensitivities offline from action-perturbed policy rollouts. Given interaction history and a policy proposal, it predicts task-relevant geometry as a nominal trajectory plus a linear response to corrections. It predicts the full planning horizon directly, avoiding the propagation of intermediate errors through recursive model evaluations. Quadratic penalties on predicted task errors and linear correction constraints then yield a cheap convex quadratic program (QP). Online refinement requires one model evaluation and one QP solve, while both policy and model weights remain frozen. Across four simulated manipulation tasks, our framework achieves the highest success rate among evaluated baselines, adding only 3.8 ms of mean refinement overhead per control step, excluding base-policy sampling.

</details>

Abstract: Embodied world models (WMs) are implicit learning systems that predict the next future latent state directly from observations and actions. Unique from other end-to-end learning systems, the dynamics predictor of WMs can simulate the environment's response to alternative actions without physical deployment nor pre-specifying states of the dynamical system. Current metrics focus on the quality of model outputs, e.g., clarity of reconstructions or task success, but fail to assess the fidelity of modeling consequences of counterfactual actions. Inspecting the forced response to action perturbations through sensitivity analysis is one way to audit how well WMs can predict the consequences of counterfactual actions. Deploying WMs on hardware will require new evaluation frameworks to analyze the tradeoffs between state error and accurate sensitivity prediction.

</details>

Abstract: 6-DOF pose estimation of large and heavy industrial objects, such as large piping systems in shipyards and structural components in petrochemical plants, remains relatively underexplored in robotic perception. Repeatedly repositioning large objects is physically and spatially demanding, making it difficult to acquire real training data with sufficient pose diversity. We fabricate a scaled-down replica and capture RGB-D data of it in diverse poses using a real RGB-D camera. RGB images are used directly, while depth maps are converted to a geometrically consistent representation of the full-scale object via a simple inverse scaling transformation. This allows training to incorporate real appearance and sensor measurement characteristics that are difficult to reproduce using synthetic data alone. To examine whether the benefit depends on a particular network, we evaluate two RGB-D pose estimators with different formulations, PVN3D and RDPN6D. Mixing at most 1,000 miniature images per pipe type, less than 10\% of the training data, with 10,000 synthetic images improves the mean ADD-S AUC across pipe types over synthetic-only training for both estimators. A comparison with training on full-scale real data further suggests that miniature data can serve as a proxy where full-scale collection is difficult, reducing the data-collection burden for large industrial objects.

Abstract: Real-world robot imitation learning is often limited by the cost of collecting physical demonstrations, which typically yield only one action label per visited state. We present CrowdMAPS, a web-based crowdsourcing paradigm for augmenting real-robot datasets with Multiple Actions per State (MAPS). After or during standard robot data collection, CrowdMAPS distributes visited robot states to remote annotators through a web-based 3D interface, where they specify alternative valid actions without requiring access to physical robot hardware. This expands robot datasets along a complementary "width" dimension--by increasing the number of actions per visited state--rather than only by collecting more trajectories. We evaluate CrowdMAPS using keyframe-centric goal-pose annotations supplied by non-expert annotators and train imitation policies on the resulting MAPS data. These results show that software-mediated human annotations can augment physical robot data to afford denser supervision, establishing CrowdMAPS as a scalable digital layer for real-robot data collection.

Abstract: Open-world navigation requires a robot to locate a user-specified target in an indoor scene under open-vocabulary queries. Realistic indoor environments are semi-static: walls and furniture largely persist while smaller objects move, relocate, or vanish between visits. Prior-map systems answer queries from object poses recorded at reconstruction time, which expire as soon as objects move, whereas online methods re-derive where to look from current observations every few steps, often through expensive calls to a vision--language model. Assuming that walls and large furniture do not move, we keep their map to plan paths, but use a target--furniture co-occurrence prior to decide where to search. We present PreNav, an ObjectNav framework on semi-static environments. Rather than invoking a vision-language model at each step, PreNav generates a single global search reference before the robot moves: an LLM converts target--furniture co-occurrence into a spatial prior, a conditional diffusion model turns that prior and the persistent structure into an ordered centerline with a band around it, and the robot approaches the target once it is detected online. Experiments on a Gibson ObjectNav benchmark show that PreNav outperforms a strong online baseline under matched structural priors, using a generator trained only on room-level ScanNet++ scenes and applied to floor-level Gibson without fine-tuning. Real-world experiments under object relocation and open-vocabulary queries further validate the approach.

</details>

Abstract: When robots interact with elastic surfaces such as mattresses and trampolines, changes in loading cause the surfaces to deform and rebound, altering subsequent contact locations and forces. We present Elastra, a simulation framework for modeling this interaction and supporting the training and evaluation of robot control policies. Elastra represents each surface using elements that move only vertically, with elastic connections and damping to model deformation and recovery. We systematically validate our simulations against an independent implementation and a real mattress load curve. We further use Elastra to train the Unitree G1 humanoid to stand up on mattresses and trampolines. Using the pretrained HoST policy and ProtoMotions motion tracker as original controllers, we train residual policies through reinforcement learning to add corrections to their joint targets. Adding these residual policies increases the success rate with HoST from 44.1\% to 72.5\%, and from 55\% to 89\% on the trampoline. Drawing on four years of large-scale development and real-world demonstrations across multiple vehicle platforms in different locations, we identify ten key challenges that hinder the development of weather-resilient autonomy, including data scarcity, domain shifts, limitations in multimodal perception, control robustness, and validation gaps, among others. We summarize these challenges and lessons learned toward enabling reliable snow-autonomous driving, highlighting critical directions for future research and standardization.

Abstract: We present DroneDiffusion, a perception-aware diffusion planner for local 3-D UAV trajectory generation that explicitly models and avoids failure zones caused by lens flare, overexposure, and reflective surfaces. A vision-language perceptual safety estimator produces a pixel-aligned reliability map that highlights regions of degraded sensing. A denoising diffusion probabilistic model (DDPM) is conditioned on this map together with the onboard RGB image, goal point, and initial velocity to generate egocentric 3-D trajectories. To enforce obstacle avoidance at inference time without retraining, we apply training-free CHOMP guidance over a 3-D Euclidean signed distance field. The model is trained entirely in simulation and deployed zero-shot on a real drone. Reliability conditioning raises the mean perceptual reliability along generated trajectories to 0.71, compared with 0.63 for a geometric baseline, while guidance halves the collision rate of the learned prior from 14.7% to 7.8%. Perceptual safety conditioning and geometric guidance are therefore complementary: treating sensor reliability as a first-class signal reduces incursions into unreliable regions while maintaining competitive navigation performance.

</details>

Abstract: Navigating off-road trails on a mountain bike requires diverse locomotion skills, including aerial maneuvers, cornering, and slip recovery, paired with sophisticated perception for scene understanding. Translating these capabilities to autonomous, two-wheeled robots remains a formidable challenge. We show that autonomous, high-speed trail navigation can be achieved through a single idea: encoding the trail as a semantic layer of the terrain. In simulation, this layer embeds the navigation task into the training environment; during deployment, it is reconstructed onboard from camera and LiDAR data. First, we introduce a unified learning framework that treats local navigation and locomotion as a joint optimization problem, bypassing the twist or pose command interface of classical control hierarchies. With fixed reward weights, the same framework produces both trail racing and agile parkour, shaped only through terrain semantics and episode length. Second, our perception pipeline combines a frozen vision foundation model with a lightweight dual-head decoder that predicts per-pixel terrain semantics and confidence scores, which are fused with LiDAR range measurements into an egocentric semantic elevation map at 30 Hz onboard an NVIDIA AGX Orin. We validate our framework on the Ultra Mobility Vehicle (UMV), a bicycle robot that autonomously raced across all sections of a kilometer-long, unstructured mountain bike trail at speeds exceeding 5 m/s and averaging 3.26 m/s. In simulation, our policy fails up to an order of magnitude less often than hierarchical baselines while driving faster.

Abstract: Vision-based high-speed navigation requires a trajectory planner that can reactively generate smooth, collision-free trajectories from onboard visual observations. Deep reinforcement learning offers a promising approach to vision-based trajectory planning, as it enables the optimization of sequential decision-making policies in complex environments. However, model-free policy gradient methods rely on sample-based policy gradient estimation, which yields noisy gradient estimates and inefficient training. To this end, we propose a framework for learning trajectory planning policies using the analytic policy gradient (APG) method and use it to train APG-Planner, a vision-based high-speed trajectory planner. The proposed framework leverages differential flatness to analytically reconstruct vehicle states from planned trajectories, thereby enabling differentiable trajectory-level state transitions without requiring an explicit low-level dynamics model. By iteratively performing trajectory planning and differentiable state transitions, we construct a differentiable episode rollout and analytically compute policy gradients from episode-level losses, improving both training efficiency and policy performance. After approximately 30 minutes of training on a single GPU, APG-Planner demonstrates comparable or superior performance to existing baseline methods in simulation and achieves speeds of up to 11\,m/s in real-world flight experiments. We further validate the proposed framework on a skid-steer ground robot, demonstrating its applicability to differentially flat robotic systems beyond quadrotors.

Abstract: Bimanual robotic manipulation is versatile, yet its central challenge lies not only in executing dexterous motion with each hand, but also in coordinating the two arms for long-horizon manipulation under coupled spatial and temporal constraints. Prior work has largely focused on endowing robots with human-level manipulation skills, paying less attention to the structure of bimanual behaviour itself: many practical tasks are a coordinated composition of reusable unimanual skills and a small set of synchronous bimanual skills. This makes high-level spatio-temporal coordination, rather than skill acquisition alone, a key bottleneck for efficient bimanual execution. We propose BiPOP, a framework that couples Large Language Models (LLMs) with Temporal Partial-Order Planning for long-horizon bimanual manipulation. At its core is an explicit spatio-temporal model that partitions the workspace by arm reachability and captures inter-arm dependencies, arm assignment, synchronization requirements, and opportunities for safe concurrent execution. Building on this model, an LLM translates natural-language instructions into a formal PDDL specification, while a temporal planner enforces the constraints and schedules actions, yielding plans that are logically consistent at the symbolic level and efficient through parallel execution. On simulated long-horizon tasks of varying complexity, BiPOP outperforms open-loop planning with strong reasoning models and closed-loop ReAct-style planning in success rate, planning time, and plan efficiency, and the full pipeline runs end to end on the real bimanual robot NICOL. A failure analysis further shows that LLM-only planners mainly violate spatial and temporal coordination constraints, which motivates delegating these constraints to a symbolic planner. Abstract: Granular materials exhibit complex and highly variable interaction dynamics, making their reliable manipulation challenging without knowledge of their physical properties. We present an interactive perception framework that infers granular material properties directly from force-torque measurements acquired during scooping. The proposed Latent Filter++ combines a bio-inspired frequency-temporal filter-bank representation with a causal latent dynamical model, allowing the representation to evolve as interaction observations become available while avoiding hand-crafted signal preprocessing. The learned latent representation captures physically meaningful variation in granular materials and supports estimation of properties including grain size and bulk density. Evaluated on 26 diverse granular materials, the approach generalizes to materials excluded from training and enables few-shot adaptation of property-specific mappings using a small number of additional interaction samples. Finally, we demonstrate the relevance of the inferred properties to downstream manipulation by examining their correlation with the maximum acceleration the materials can withstand without spilling from the spoon during transport.

Abstract: World Action Models (WAMs) can improve robot learning by jointly predicting future visual states and actions, but generating a visual future at every replanning step is expensive and sometimes unnecessary. This raises a critical question: when should a robot policy imagine the future? In this paper, we propose Bind-WAM, a binding-uncertainty-guided WAM for selective future imagination. Bind-WAM introduces language-object binding uncertainty, selectively invoking joint future-action generation when the current observation does not support stable task-relevant action generation. An offline labeling pipeline uses complete demonstrations and action context to identify task-relevant objects and detect reference mismatch, invisibility, and identity drift. The resulting pseudo-labels supervise a lightweight router that predicts binding uncertainty from the current observation and language instruction. At inference, a shared WAM policy invokes selective future imagination according to the predicted uncertainty and its change between consecutive replanning steps. Simulation and real-world experiments demonstrate that Bind-WAM can establish binding uncertainty as a strong, interpretable signal for selective future imagination.

</details>

Abstract: Industrial manipulators offer reach and precision but can damage nearby structures in confined spaces. Soft robots provide compliant contact but typically have limited reach. Rigid-soft systems combine their advantages, although they often require dedicated actuation and control hardware. We propose actuating a passive tendon-driven soft extension through manipulator joint motion. As the robot moves, the exterior spans of tendons anchored to upstream links change, altering tendon tension and deforming the extension. Inspection through narrow apertures, for example, constrains the motion of the end effector (EE). We develop a controller that regulates the soft tip while preserving such EE constraints. A differential model separates rigid transport from soft deformation, combining analytic arm and tendon kinematics with a locally identified span-to-deformation map. We also derive a local upper bound on the number of independent deformation directions transmitted by the tendons under the EE constraint, and conditions for the exponential convergence of the tip error. A quadratic program with control barrier functions imposes obstacle clearance, fulcrum limits, and model-validity conditions. Simulations and real experiments show tip and shape regulation under free, position-constrained, and fulcrum-constrained EE motion.

Abstract: Existing methods for predicting surrounding vehicles' forward occupancy may under-approximate the ground-truth occupancy, exposing the ego vehicle to collision risk, or produce overly conservative regions that unnecessarily restrict feasible space and cause planning failures. To address these issues, we propose FloCOS, a conditional flow-matching framework that predicts compact forward occupancy sets with high occupancy coverage. FloCOS represents the forward occupancy set as a time-varying union of half-space convex polygons parameterized by temporal basis coefficients. Conditional flow matching captures the conditional distribution of these coefficients to govern the temporal evolution of the convex components, trained under coverage and union-area losses that penalize missed occupancy and excess area. For downstream trajectory optimization, the predicted nonconvex union is directly incorporated into hyperplane-based collision-avoidance constraints. Evaluations on the Waymo Open Motion Dataset show that FloCOS generates compact forward occupancy sets with high coverage and achieves a higher interaction completion rate than evaluated baselines. Real-world vehicle experiments further confirm its ability to support collision-free planning in interactive scenarios.

</details>

Abstract: Most action tokenizers for autoregressive vision language action (VLA) models compress motion into chunks that must be decoded in full before their actions become executable. We introduce ActionCodec2, a non-neural streaming tokenizer that releases actions incrementally while keeping trajectory reconstruction error within a prescribed tolerance. Its encoder quantizes each step relative to the decoder's reconstructed state, so the selected motion primitive corrects residual error rather than allowing errors from independently quantized increments to accumulate. Finding the smallest such primitive set is a geometric covering problem; we derive the exact minimum for translation and constant-factor-tight bounds for rotation. Set-BPE then losslessly merges co-occurring primitive symbols across coordinates and neighboring steps, without a fixed coordinate ordering. A universal version fitted on 20k hours of data from 24 embodiments averages 8.8 tokens per second for 15 Hz end-effector motion on this corpus. With the same VLA backbone, ActionCodec2 outperforms the compared tokenizers on three simulation benchmarks and in zero-shot real-robot evaluation. Its streaming representation allows policies to incorporate state feedback within an action chunk, substantially improving performance over open-loop execution under randomized dynamics, external disturbances, and moving objects. The same tokens also can serve as a direct control interface for VLM agents, which compose successful action sequences into reusable macros. These agent-built macros reduce control decisions and thinking-token usage across attempts and transfer to a held-out task. More results are available at https://zibindong.github.io/actioncodec2-page/

</details>

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) models predict chunks of future actions, and when objects move, those chunks go stale before they are executed. We study what makes a pretrained VLA reactive, using \pihalf{} on two dynamic simulation benchmarks and three real-robot tasks. We vary the prediction and execution horizons, compare synchronous and asynchronous inference, test methods that deliver actions sooner or keep successive chunks continuous, and adapt the policy with motion cues and latent-space reinforcement learning. The best prediction horizon is about one second, and the best execution horizon depends on the checkpoint. Short horizons help more with asynchronous execution, and methods that keep the trajectory continuous outperform those that only reduce latency. Adapting the policy repairs targeted failures, but on the conveyor task changing the execution horizon gained as much. Reactivity depends on the execution pipeline as much as on the policy, so both should be evaluated together.

</details>

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models have demonstrated strong performance in robotic manipulation, yet most rely primarily on 2D visual observations and language instructions, leaving scene geometry and obstacle relationships to be inferred implicitly. This limitation is particularly problematic in cluttered environments, where small spatial errors can lead to unintended contacts or collisions. We propose MOSAIC, a modular spatial- and obstacle-aware framework that can be integrated with pretrained VLA policies to improve object-level scene understanding and action generation. Our method augments the VLA backbone with an auxiliary semantic grounding task that predicts role-specific masks for the target object, destination region, and surrounding obstacles. We further combine the target and destination masks with depth estimated from 3D scene reconstruction to construct role-specific geometric tokens that are injected directly into the action expert. In addition, we propose obstacle-aware attention supervision that emphasizes obstacles near the robot body during task execution. The resulting attention features are subsequently injected into later action-expert layers to further guide action generation. Together, these components provide explicit semantic, geometric, and obstacle-aware cues while preserving the underlying pretrained VLA architecture. We evaluate our framework on pretrained VLA model using the RoboPRO benchmark, including visual perturbation tests, and further validate it on real-world manipulation tasks. MOSAIC improves the success rate by 5.1% in cluttered scenes, and by 9.7% under visual perturbations, with gains of up to 25.0% in the most challenging environment. Abstract: This work addresses the problem of finding a concealed object in an environment by unifying formalisms for partially observable environments with multi-modal priors drawn from a mix of ontologies, semantic networks, LLMs, and VLMs. We propose a concealment-adaptive mixed-observability MDP (CA-MOMDP), a Bayes-adaptive setting where uncertainty is over both object locations and which nodes can conceal them, held as a hypothesis with a bounded ontology prior and resolved only by interaction. We define a hierarchical transition system (HTS) to abstract the environment into room and object nodes that can be planned over via our CA-MOMDP. We further propose a COMBS-score: a multi-modal fusion of a human informed semantic network, LLM, and VLM, to initialize the belief over object locations, which grounds our abstractions and reduces reliance on a single model. Belief is maintained via Bayes' rule, which never lets a look rule out a closed container. We prove a present target is found in a bounded number of searches under exact perception and that its location is never ruled out given missed detection under noisy perception. We ablate each component to demonstrate their significance. Across five simulator configurations, from a gridworld to Matterport3D with a real detector, COMBS finds 92-100% of in-distribution and 92-100% of out-of-distribution targets, matches or beats the baselines in processing speed, significantly outperforms a per-step LLM policy at lower cost on the larger scenes, recovers more out-of-distribution placements than a TidyBot ranking, and matches an LLM-only prior at a fraction of its per-decision latency. COMBS is the only standalone method that can declare a target absent at a stated error level, and the per-step LLM policy offered a termination action abandons 12-22% of targets that were present, against 1.6-3.2% for COMBS on the same episodes.

Abstract: Multi-camera observations in off-road environments vary significantly in reliability, posing challenges when complementing degraded geometric constraints. The front end evaluates camera observations through read-only pretracking on a shared lidar-inertial posterior, combining observation health with geometric complementarity. Channel health memory and switching hysteresis govern selection of at most one eligible view for direct photometric correction. Across 12 controlled camera-fault configurations involving dropout, black frames and 60 ms timestamp shifts, it maintained complete trajectories with errors of 1.54-2.23 m. These runtime results demonstrate the real-time feasibility of selective visual state updates alongside multi-view semantic mapping under challenging off-road conditions. Abstract: Many robotic applications involve scenarios where multiple robots interact and the decisions of one robot influence the behavior of others. This paper presents a quadratic programming (QP)-based feedback controller for multi-robot conflict resolution with input constraints. Safety is enforced using coupled Control Barrier Function (CBF), while a yielding matrix encodes how robots influence each other. Unlike existing methods with fixed or hard priorities, the proposed framework enables yielding preferences while guaranteeing deadlock-free operation by design. Sufficient conditions are established for the controller to be locally Lipschitz. The optimal CBF sign- indefinite slack is shown to admit a finite uniform bound, which is compensated through an enlarged CBF safety margin to obtain a forward invariant set contained within the desired safe set. The analysis also identifies an irreducible slack component due to finite maximum deceleration that cannot be eliminated by increasing the slack penalty. Finally, sufficient conditions on the yielding matrix and nominal controller are derived to exclude deadlock states. Simulations demonstrate adaptive coordination across yielding preferences and robustness to robot failures.

Abstract: We study robot navigation in indoor environments from fixed exocentric cameras (infrastructure-mounted, external to the robot). We show that standard Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO), which succeeds with onboard cameras, fails to learn this navigation end-to-end in our setup. This failure results from the perspective mismatch between exocentric observations and egocentric action and goal formulations, which is too complex to resolve from the reward signal alone. To address this, we propose Viewpoint-Invariant Navigation from Exocentric Cameras (VINE). VINE uses an obstacle map estimation network, trained with supervised learning, that takes exocentric images, camera pose, robot pose, and room dimensions as input and produces a viewpoint-invariant obstacle map. This map can be used either by a PPO policy or by a planner such as A*. VINE's map estimation performs better than perspective mapping approaches, handles cameras placed across a wide range of positions and orientations without view-specific tuning, requires only approximate camera pose estimates (noise with a standard deviation of 30cm per axis in x, y, and z and about 20° of rotation error on average), is robust to intrinsic variation, and extends to two-camera setups with partial coverage. Despite operating from cameras randomized in position during evaluation, VINE surpasses the navigation success rate of PPO with an onboard camera in simulated environments.

Abstract: Robots handle much more than rigid objects: towels, cables, water, sand. The simulators used to train robots at scale are strongest on rigid bodies, and adding another material usually means a second engine and hand-written glue between the two. We present Alloy, an open-source physics engine for robot learning in which rigid robots, cloth, cables, liquids, granular material and soft bodies share one scene. Each object is described once; Alloy assigns it to a solver suited to its material, couples the solvers inside every control step, and runs the scene in many parallel worlds on one GPU, or on a CPU. Every world carries a health grade, to help tell a failure of the physics from a failure of the policy. Alloy is built on Newton, MuJoCo Warp and Warp, and it is written to be driven by coding agents: its documentation ships inside the package, every error names its fix, and the engine is exposed as tools over the Model Context Protocol. In a cold test, agents that had never seen Alloy installed a pre-release from the package index and trained policies for walking, cable handling and pouring on a single GPU. We also report how throughput grows with the number of worlds, depth-camera throughput, agreement with closed-form references, and a reliability test over a thousand worlds.

</details>

Abstract: At disaster sites and on unstable slopes, robot contact can collapse uninspected terrain, requiring retreat upon support loss. Massively parallel reinforcement learning of collapse-reactive retreat requires independent state--action experiences. However, on shared rigid-body collapsible terrain, one robot's collapse alters others' contacts without direct interaction. We call this indirect interference. Duplicating terrain objects prevents this but is memory-intensive, limiting parallel sampling. Our key insight is that policy learning does not necessarily require rigid-body collapse modeling, which is feasible but computationally expensive; collapse-induced contact responses can suffice. We therefore propose a memory-efficient parallel policy-learning framework for collapse-reactive retreat via hybrid terrain modeling. Hybrid terrain modeling combines rigid-body simulation of non-collapsible terrain with virtual contact forces for collapsible regions. A State-Multiplexed Contact Model shares terrain geometry and a contact-response law while maintaining environment-specific collapse states, enabling independent experiences without terrain objects for collapsible regions. The framework trains a proprioceptive policy for both normal locomotion and collapse-reactive retreat. It supports over 100 times as many parallel environments as fine-resolution rigid-block terrain within the same GPU memory limit. This scalability and improved sample efficiency enable over 50 times faster policy convergence with comparable retreat performance. Sim-to-sim and real-robot experiments demonstrate retreat under multiple collapse conditions. A supplementary video is available at: https://youtu.be/_RrhwjzEPE0

</details>

Abstract: Reusing a demonstrated gait can reduce the work of specifying the same motion for each biped. A video captures the timing of a step, but each robot must execute it with its own limbs and actuators. MotionSkill recovers a timed reference once from monocular video and adapts it to each target in simulation. It aligns root motion and body landmarks in a common frame and records which landmarks are available at each instant. At each source timestamp, the mapper preserves limb directions, uses the target segment lengths, and solves inverse kinematics under joint limits and closure constraints. In MuJoCo, we train a separate residual policy for each of ten bipeds on its mapped reference from one four-second walking clip. Each policy uses the measured robot state to correct joint commands during contact. From initial states held out during training, MotionSkill completed 98% of trials on the original video and 26% with the upper body occluded for one second.

Abstract: Recent advances in robotic manipulation have enabled increasingly complex and dexterous behaviors, yet existing benchmarks still focus on simple pick-and-place tasks and lack a systematic framework for measuring progress in robot learning along the axis of task difficulty. We present ManiLadder, a large-scale and organized simulation benchmark for robotic manipulation. ManiLadder contains 112 carefully designed tasks stratified into 4 difficulty levels, with 8 categories in each level, spanning rigid-body and deformable-object manipulation, single-arm and dual-arm settings, and gripper and dexterous-hand end-effectors. To calibrate difficulty, we fix the data amount the same for every task and use a unified flow- matching imitation learning policy under the same training and evaluation protocols, then use its evaluation success rate as an operational empirical difficulty signal. All tasks are iteratively refined until they fall into target difficulty ranges, which are then verified across different random seeds, policies, and agentic learning systems. We further analyze the task properties, compare different policy performances and training settings, analyze task transfer and data scaling behaviors, and conduct real-world experiments showing that the designed tasks preserve their difficulty ordering beyond simulation. Abstract: In long-horizon manipulation, a robot may need to take different actions under identical observations, depending on task progress and the outcomes of earlier attempts. We refer to this ambiguity as decision aliasing. Resolving it requires the policy to maintain memory of its execution history. To reduce the cost of processing full observation histories, recent methods build compact event memories using perceptual, semantic, or kinematic cues. However, these criteria may miss events that matter for task success. We instead propose an outcome-grounded principle: remember events that substantially change the predicted probability of eventual success. Following this principle, we introduce CGEM, a critic-gated event memory that uses a critic to select events with large estimated changes in success probability. Positive and negative changes are encoded as progress and failure records, respectively. Failure records are discarded once the corresponding subtask is completed. In a ten-subtask diagnostic environment with exactly computed success probabilities, CGEM achieves 88.0% success, compared with 0.0% for both a 36-step observation window and a kinematic event memory. Removing failure records reduces success to 1.7%.

</details>

Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) have become a powerful foundation for robotic scene understanding, task planning, and language-conditioned control. A natural question follows: can these models also decode what a human operator intends while teleoperating a robot, without any explicit instruction? To study this, we introduce VI AMO (Vision Intention Action and Mission Ongoing), a dataset of 6,600 episodes from eleven robot sources with 13,653 temporally aligned action annotations and 9,702 mission annotations. Large proprietary models struggle with this problem: on held-out episodes, GPT-4o reaches Sentence-BERT similarities of only 0.563 and 0.470 for action and mission, and GPT-5.6 Luna achieves only 0.581 and 0.554. Finally, as a downstream application, VIM's predicted mission enables a vision-language-action (VLA) policy to take over from the operator mid-task in a real-world environment unseen during VIM fine-tuning, reducing human teleoperation time by 34.6% relative to GPT-4o and 39.8% relative to GPT-5.6 Luna, while cutting intent-prediction latency by roughly 70% on average.

Abstract: Whole-body motion tracking lets a humanoid robot follow reference motions, but training a tracking policy for a library of motions has usually taken hours of GPU time. SuperMimic showed that the policy can instead be trained by backpropagating through a world model learned from parallel simulation, and InstantTrack added Muon updates, a residual policy around the reference and an averaged deployed policy. We build on both and present BlinkMimic, a recipe that makes this training faster. On a simulated Unitree G1, BlinkMimic trains one policy for all 40 LAFAN1 clips in 3.4 minutes on a single RTX 4090, measured until three consecutive checkpoints reach a normalized return of at least 0.82 with no failed clip (mean of three seeds). We split training time into the cost of an iteration, the iterations needed to fit the motions, and the iterations needed to remove the last failures, and assign each component, inherited or new, to one of these terms. The new components are a step size that decreases once training has progressed, resets directed by failures of the deployed policy under deployment conditions, and GPU graph capture of the whole iteration. A leave-one-out ablation with three seeds per component shows which term each component affects, and further measurements of the gradient, the update rule and the failure cases explain these effects. Abstract: Vision-based dexterous in-hand manipulation requires knowing not only where an object is, but also how it moves. However, most robotic manipulation systems rely on off-the-shelf visual perception models whose outputs are limited to static object masks, geometry, or 6D poses, leaving downstream controllers to infer object dynamics implicitly from noisy visual observations. We present VOKE, a kinematics-aware perception framework that augments existing object and hand-object pose estimators to recover a complete object kinematic state comprising pose, linear and angular velocity, and linear and angular acceleration. VOKE contains two core perception modules and a downstream application study. First, a Pluggable Kinematics Module (PKM) attaches to the output of a standard pose estimator and predicts the object's linear and angular velocity and acceleration from temporal visual features. Second, a Kinematics-guided Pose Refinement Module (KPRM) converts this motion vector into a second-order, motion-prior reference pose and refines the per-frame pose estimates. We evaluate VOKE on DexYCB and OakInk, where it consistently improves object pose estimation across three different backbones and substantially reduces velocity and acceleration estimation error compared with pose differencing. As a downstream application, we use Kinematics-Augmented Reinforcement Learning (KARL) to compare a kinematics-augmented policy with a pose-history baseline. After training the policies in simulation, we transfer the kinematics-augmented policy to the physical robot without policy fine-tuning, demonstrating the complete perception-to-control pathway in the target domain.

</details>

Abstract: Learning whole-body mobile manipulation requires demonstrations that coordinate bimanual actions with locomotion across extended workspaces. However, existing interfaces trade off direct robot control, perception and action alignment, and natural operator mobility, making such demonstrations difficult to collect. We introduce HALO, a human-aligned data collection system for whole-body mobile manipulation. The system aligns the operator's perception and action space with the robot while preserving direct physical feedback and natural locomotion. Operators guide the robot end-effectors through kinesthetic teaching and move a low-resistance platform without the deployed mobile base while performing bimanual manipulation. This interface enables more natural whole-body demonstrations, but the close human-robot coupling introduces discrepancies between the demonstration and deployment settings. We address these discrepancies with deployment-aligned demonstration processing, which calibrates and temporally aligns platform motion with the deployed base response and masks and inpaints demonstrator hands in recorded observations. Compared with Mobile ALOHA and VR teleoperation, HALO enables up to 2.9x faster demonstration collection while maintaining comparable or higher success rates. Policies trained with HALO perform precise manipulation tasks, including zip-tie fastening, and achieve a 73.3% success rate on a six-stage long-horizon mobile manipulation task involving repeated coordinated base and arm motion. Abstract: Low-impedance grippers make the dynamics of a grasp matter. Current grasp metrics, however, are static: they describe how a held object resists constant loads, not how it responds to time-varying ones. To quantify this, we rely on small-signal analysis to characterize a grasp by its admittance, the transfer matrix relating load perturbations to object displacements around an equilibrium. This transfer matrix recovers the stiffness and stability of the grasp at zero frequency and captures its natural frequencies and peak gain under time-varying loads. We apply this characterization to a planar two-finger gripper under proportional-derivative control. We show that two grasps of the same object in the same position and with the same contact forces can differ in stiffness by two orders of magnitude. Likewise, two grasps that share the same stiffness can exhibit very different resonances. Nonlinear simulations confirm the effectiveness of our small-signal analysis in capturing the dynamic behavior of grasping.

</details>

Abstract: Actions in expert demonstrations exhibit temporal structure. An ordered-versus-shuffled analysis across five environments confirms this dependence. We ask whether action proposals that better reflect expert continuity can improve visual world-model planning. Rather than imposing generic smoothness, we learn continuations from the temporal structure of demonstrated actions. Our locality-gated deep-kernel Gaussian-process prior, LG-DKGP, combines recent and longer-range executed action history, predicts a neural continuation, and learns a temporal Gaussian-process (GP) residual. A training-derived exploration scale initializes search around the corrected mean; a frozen world model evaluates progress toward the goal. Training first fits the neural mean, then jointly optimizes the prior with the GP residual using expert future actions. The resulting planner achieves 87.07\% macro success across five simulated tasks spanning native and fragment-level goal attainment, compared with 71.03\% for INTACT, 61.66\% for PRISM, and 58.66\% for LeWM. Component interventions and expert-relative action-structure comparisons support demonstration-informed continuations and reveal task-dependent effects of visual conditioning, history length, and planning horizon.

Abstract: Planning for a heterogeneous robot team from a scene image and an instruction involves two kinds of decisions. Some must be judged from the scene: which goals are already met, how the task splits, and which robot does what. Others follow from these and can be computed: prerequisite actions, handoffs between robots, and timing. We argue that each kind should go to the component suited to it: a small vision-language model learns the first, and a deterministic solver computes the second. In NSDL (Neuro-Symbolic Division of Labor), the trained model writes its judgments as a plan sketch, which lists the goals that still need action and the intended steps in order, each with a robot. The solver expands the sketch into an executable joint plan. The model calls two tools, a detector for evidence and the solver, and is trained with the solver in the loop. We evaluate on the public VIKI-L2 benchmark and on LOCUS-Bench, a new benchmark that grades the two kinds of difficulty separately. NSDL outperforms end-to-end planners and formalize-then-plan baselines on both benchmarks and with three backbones from 3B to 8B; the 7B model reaches 97.0% out-of-distribution accuracy on VIKI-L2. Ordering and scheduling errors nearly disappear once the solver writes the structure; the remaining failures are misjudged states, and most goals the model misses are ones it never lists. Code, model weights, and LOCUS-Bench are publicly available at https://github.com/Xincheng-Tan/NSDL.

</details>

Abstract: Recent advances in frontier models have pushed the boundaries of agentic robot control, enabling agents to perform complex robotic tasks through reasoning and interaction. However, repeated calls to these models during execution can substantially increase task completion time. Code as Policies (CaP) offers a route for coding agents to acquire physical skills through experience and retain them in executable programs, allowing the reasoning invested in development to be reused across runs. Developing reliable code policies while limiting the cost of physical experimentation remains a central challenge. We investigate human guidance as a source of priors for feasible actions and simulation as an auxiliary environment for exploring and testing candidate policies. Our findings favor robot demonstrations available on request and simulation used selectively during real-world policy refinement. These findings inform ACE-CaP-0, an agentic recipe that couples policy revision with experience acquisition through Demonstrations on Request and Simulation on Demand. Abstract: Accurate environmental monitoring relies on Informative Path Planning (IPP) to deploy autonomous systems that maximize information gain. While Gaussian Processes (GPs) provide a robust mathematical foundation for modeling the environment, contemporary IPP approaches remain hindered by heuristic operational constraints and the lack of simultaneous theoretical guarantees for worst-case estimation error and approximation quality. Rather than introducing yet another heuristic, this paper presents a rigorous theoretical analysis of existing error bounds and algorithms to further develop the mathematical foundation for IPP.
  By evaluating probabilistic and deterministic bounds on reconstruction error, we establish that predictive variance is the theoretically ideal optimization objective for GP-based IPP. Furthermore, we analyze GreedyCover, a recent approach for optimizing predictive variance, and demonstrate that its near-optimal approximation guarantee matches the fundamental hardness-of-approximation limit for the problem. Comprehensive benchmarking confirms that this approach delivers superior estimation accuracy and computational efficiency compared to prominent information-theoretic objectives and optimization methods used in IPP. Finally, this theoretical analysis establishes a rigorous, near-optimal foundation for discrete offline IPP under bounded uncertainty constraints, offering clear and principled guidance for future work in continuous-space and online formulations.

Abstract: State-of-the-art LiDAR-inertial odometry methods, including those on richer state manifolds, achieve strong global accuracy, yet their pose stream alternates between propagation and correction and can oscillate over short horizons even when globally accurate. Most existing methods follow these approaches: they either modify the filter to enable faster updates or estimate a continuous-time trajectory from raw LiDAR and inertial data. The latter involves extra optimization, delaying state updates and becoming less robust during rapid rotations or accelerations. We propose CT-SGAL-LIO, a continuous-time refinement module that smooths the posterior pose stream of a discrete SGal(3) estimator without modifying the estimator itself. The key novelty is to perform continuous-time trajectory refinement directly on the projected SGal(3) posterior: we fit a cubic trajectory over a bounded window of recent poses and evaluate it at a small fixed lag. This places the query time within the observed interval, so the refined pose is obtained by interpolation rather than extrapolation, while preserving causal operation. CT-SGAL-LIO improves relative trajectory accuracy on 17 sequences from three public benchmarks. Across 17 sequences from GRAND-TOUR, MCD, and GEODE, CT-SGAL-LIO improves short-term motion accuracy while largely preserving global accuracy. RTE improves by up to 70% on GRAND-TOUR and on all seven GEODE sequences, with 9.9-51.1% reductions. A 19.5-min R-Campus run also reduces posterior jumps by 85.4% with only a 4.7 mm change in the end-to-start gap.
